Now the crucial part: In ray_create, after the copy, sp48..56 = point (not cam!). Wait no, we determined sp48..56 = cam originally, then overwritten by the copy of sp32..40 (point). Yes.

Hmm, so dst.origin = point, dst.dir = normalize(point). That means the ray tracer starts rays from the image plane point and shoots toward the camera origin (normalize(point) points from origin toward point...). Let's compute: point = (aspect(u+0.5), 1-2v, -1). normalize(point) points roughly in direction of point from origin. A ray starting at point going in direction normalize(point) moves away from origin, toward -z (since point.z=-1). So rays go from the image plane into the -z direction, away from camera.

But geometry: camera at origin, image plane at z=-1 (in front... all points have z=-1). A "normal" renderer would cast rays from origin through pixels at z=-1, so rays travel in -z direction from z=0 through z=-1 toward scene at z<-1. This matches! The ray params: camera origin = (0,0,0), image plane at z=-1; ray through pixel P has origin O=(0,0,0) and direction P=(x_pixel, y_pixel, -1). 

But ray_create sets origin=P and dir=normalize(P) — the ray is then P + t*normalize(P). At t=0, point is P (on image plane), and for t>0 it continues in the -z direction away from camera. That's equivalent to the same ray as origin=O with direction P, except parametrized differently: The standard ray R(t) = O + t*P. The implemented ray R'(s) = P + s*normalize(P). These two represent the same line (both pass through O and P) but with different parametrization: R'(s) = P + s*P/|P|. So the line is the same, just the parameter t scales. For intersection, the code tests the sphere at parameter s (ray parameter) — since origin differs, distances along the ray are measured differentlycars. This changes the shadow test (which uses the same ray from the point) but the actual intersection point is the same line.

Hmm wait, but the sphere intersection code uses the ray struct with origin at P and direction normalize(P). The distance t is in units of normalized direction. The hit test uses `t < 0.001` guard etc. The shadow test also uses ray from the surface point toward the light.

Actually, since both parametrizations represent the same line, the intersection points on the sphere/plane are the same; the parameter values differ but the code adapts. So the rendering should be equivalent to a standard raycaster from camera origin with direction P (unnormalized) as long as constants/guards are consistent.

Let me look at `sphere_intersect` details:

sphere_intersect(ray* r (x0), center (s0..s2), radius (s3), result* x8):
```
fe8: mov x20, x8       ; result
fec: fmov s5,s0; s4,s1; s1,s2; s0,s3   ; reorder center/radius
ffc: mov x19, x0       ; ray

On AArch64, `b.mi` branches if condition "minus" i.e., less than zero? `fcmpe s0, #0.0` sets flags, `b.mi` = branch if minus (negative). So if disc < 0 -> result indicates no hit (fills result at sp? with the "no" struct).

So if (floor(x)+floor(z)) is even => ceil? `cset w0, eq` sets w0=1 if comparison "eq" was true, i.e., if (x_floor+z_floor) & 1 == 0 (even). So even parity => white tile, odd => dark tile.

The tile colors:
- even: `mov w0,#0x6666; movk #0x3f66` => 0x3F666666 = 0.9f. vector=(0.9,0.9,0.9) white.
- odd: `mov w0,#0xcccc; movk #0x3ecc` => 0x3ECCCCCC = 0.4f. vector=(0.4,0.4,0.4) dark.

RGB: 0.9*255.999=230, 0.4*255.999=102.

Now, the key question: Will reimplementing this in C produce the exact same image? The float computations in the binary use float (32-bit) throughout (vector functions operate on `float`), except `sqrt`, `fmax/fmin/floor` operate on doubles via ABI, and intermediate conversions. Actually:
- `vector_length` uses sqrt as double: `fcvt d0,s0; bl sqrt@plt; fcvt s0,d0`.
- `sqrt` in sphere_intersect uses double. So sqrt results are double rounded to float.

To reproduce exactly in C we should use `float` for vector ops and `float t = sqrtf(...)`? Wait the binary calls `sqrt` (the double version) after converting float to double, then truncates back to float. If I use `sqrtf` in C the result might differ in the last bit vs `(float)sqrt((double)x)`. This could cause a ±1 pixel difference on some pixels. Since we need 0.99 similarity (very lenient), minor differences are fine.

Similarly `floor`, `fmax`, `fmin` in binary are called on doubles (`fcvt d0,s0; bl floor@plt`), returning doubles then converted to float. In C, `floorf` or `(float)floor(double)` differ possibly in last bit. Minor.

Let me now consider the bigger question: does the target image match EXACTLY the binary output, or was it downsampled? The ASCII art and sample values suggest the image is not supersampled (it has crisp aliasing). Let me verify: if it were rendered at 2400x1800 directly, edges are "hard." If downsampled from 4800x3600, the pixels near edges would have intermediate values. Let me look at specific pixels around the sphere silhouette to check for aliasing vs smooth AA.

Let me analyze the image: extract a vertical or horizontal scanline through the sphere edge and see if there's a hard step or gradual blend.

Also, the parity checkerboard uses floor(x)+floor(z). With 2400x1800 and no AA, the checkerboard edges would be hard steps. In the ASCII art we see the checkerboard looks blocky.

   1d74: ldr s0, [sp,#44]   ; W (int)
   1d78: scvtf s1, s0       ; (float)W
   1d7c: ldr s0, [sp,#48]   ; H
   1d80: scvtf s0, s0       ; (float)H
   1d84: fdiv s0, s1, s0    ; s0 = W/H = 2400/1800 = 1.3333
   1d88: ldr s1, [sp,#52]   ; s1 = 2.0
   1d8c: fmul s0, s1, s0    ; s0 = 2.66667 = aspect
   ```
   So aspect = 2.0f * (float)W / (float)H (in float). sp60 = 1.0 (unused?).

2. right = (aspect, 0, 0); up = (0, 2, 0); corner/base = (-aspect/2, -1, -1).

   - a = right * u = (aspect*u, 0, 0)
   - b = up * (1-v) = (0, 2*(1-v), 0)  [the compiler wrote (1-v) as computed: 1.0f - v]

   ```
   Note: 2*(1-v) - 1 = 2-2v-1 = 1-2v. In float: 2.0f*(1.0f-v)-1.0f. Where `1.0f-v` computed in float.

4. ray: origin = cam (0,0,0), dir = normalize(point). But as discovered, ray_create sets origin = point, dir = normalize(point) — but the scene intersection results differ in parameterization. Actually let's reconsider whether origin matters for the FINAL rendered image.

The ray line is the same either way (through cam and point). Intersection with sphere/plane yields the same hit POINT. The normal at that point is the same. The lighting dot product uses the light direction and normal — the same. The shadow test uses a ray from hit point + 0.001*normal toward light — independent of the original ray's parameterization (it creates a new ray). The checkerboard uses hit point — same. So the rendered color is the same regardless of whether ray origin is cam or point, as long as the LINE is the same. The only place where origin/parameterization might matter is in the intersection t-values, but t is only used to select nearest object between sphere and plane. Since both sphere and plane are intersected with the same ray struct (same parameterization), the nearest comparison is consistent.

Wait, is it? If ray origin = cam, dir = normalize(point): t values measured from cam along normalized dir. If ray origin = point, dir = normalize(point): t values measured from point. The SPHERE and PLANE intersections are computed using the SAME ray struct, so their relative t comparison remains the same ordering (both shifted by the same constant along the line). Actually the t comparison is `sphere_t < plane_t` for the same param; since both use same ray, ordering consistent. Let me think again: if ray origin = point on image plane and direction = normalize(point), then the ray starts at the image plane and goes AWAY from camera (into the scene). The sphere/plane intersections with t>0.001 would be found in the -z direction. This is actually the CORRECT behavior for rendering the scene in front of the camera! Because:
- Camera at origin, image plane at z=-1. Rays from camera through pixels continue into z < -1 (away from camera).
- The binary's ray starts at the image plane point P and travels in direction normalize(P) (which points from origin toward P, i.e., into -z for points with z=-1). So starting at P and moving along +dir (away from origin) goes into z<-1, toward the scene. 

So the ray origin being at the image plane is actually correct for rendering the far scene: the ray travels from image plane toward -infinity. If the origin were at the camera, it would travel from z=0 through z=-1 toward -infinity — same line, same intersections. The t parameter differs (smaller from cam). Both work.

The issue of t>=0.001 guard: with origin=point, the sphere at (0,-0.5,-5) is far (t large). No problemcars.

But before writing, let me consider the precision issues more deeply. The binary uses single-precision float arithmetic. Let me plan the C implementation to mirror operations:

- Use `float` for all vector ops.
- Use `sqrt` from math (double) — the binary calls `sqrt@plt` (double) with float input converted to doubleaisle. `(float)sqrt((double)x)`. In C, if I write `float t = sqrtf(x);` that's different from `(float)sqrt(x)`. To match exactly, I should call `sqrt` (double version) and assign to float, or `(float)sqrt(x)`. Similarly `floor` — binary uses `floor@plt` on doubles: `(float)floor((double)x)`. And `fmax/fmin` — binary uses doubles: `(float)fmax((double)x,0.0)` etc.

Actually, notice `fmax`/`fmin` in binary are used to clamp and max. They operate on doubles. I can replicate with C `fmaxf`? To match exact float, I'd use `(float)fmax(x,0.0f)`. Since fmax of float x with 0 returns exactly x if x>0. Bit-exact when using double version? `fmax` with double operands: `(double)x` and 0.0 → max value, then cast to float. Since x is already representable in float, casting to double is exact, fmax exact, casting back exact. So no precision difference for fmax.

- For `sqrt`: `(float)sqrt((double)x)` vs `sqrtf(x)` — the double sqrt gives more precision, cast back to float may round differently than sqrtf (which computes in float). For some inputs, differ in last ulp. This could affect pixel boundaries in a couple pixels. Acceptable.

- For `floor`: same reasoning.

But — big deal: will float vs double intermediate computations in our C math produce exactly the same as the binary's float computations? If we use float variables and float ops, yes mostly. The binary uses float vectors (`fadd`, `fmul`, etc. operate on 32-bit S regs). The only double ops are `sqrt`, `floor`, `fmax`, `fmin`, `fprintf` formatting. We can match by using `float` and casting for sqrt/floor.

Actually, wait: to test "in shadow" of a self-occluding sphere, you want to cast the ray toward the light source. ```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define W 2400
#define H 1800
typedef struct {float x,y,z;} V;
static V add(V a,V b){V c={a.x+b.x,a.y+b.y,a.z+b.z};return c;}
static V sub(V a,V b){V c={a.x-b.x,a.y-b.y,a.z-b.z};return c;}
static V mul(V a,float t){V c={a.x*t,a.y*t,a.z*t};return c;}
static float dot(V a,V b){return a.x*b.x+a.y*b.y+a.z*b.z;}
static float len(V a){return (float)sqrt((double)dot(a,a));}
static V norm(V a){float l=len(a);V c={a.x/l,a.y/l,a.z/l};return c;}
```

typedef struct {V o,d;} Ray;

Sphere intersect:
```
int sph(Ray r, V c, float rad, float *t, V *p, V *n){
    V L=sub(r.o,c);
    float a=dot(r.d,r.d);
    float b=dot(r.d,L)*2.0f;
    float c0=dot(L,L)-rad*rad;
    float disc=b*b-4.0f*a*c0_discrepant...
```
Wait, need to mirror the binary's exact operations:

Binary sphere_intersect:
- L = r.o - c
- a = dot(r.d, r.d)         // sp112
- b = 2*dot(r.d, L)         // sp116 = dot+dout then fadd s0,s0,s0 (x2 in float)
- c0 = dot(L,L) - rad*rad   // sp120
- disc = b*b - 4*a*c0       // sp124 = (b*b) - (4*(a*c0))? Let me check order:
```
10d0: ldr s0, sp116; fmul s1, s0, s0       ; s1 = b*b
10d8: ldr s2, sp112 (a); fmov #4; fmul s2, s2, #4   ; s2 = 4*a
10e4: ldr s0, sp120 (c0)
10e8: fmul s0, s2, s0      ; s0 = (4*a)*c0
10ec: fsub s0, s1, s0      ; disc = b*b - (4*a*c0)
```
So disc = b*b - 4.0f*a*c0 (with a*(4) then *c0). In C: `float disc = b*b - 4.0f*a*c0;` — but C evaluates `4.0f*a*c0` as (4.0f*a)*c0 (left-assoc) = same. Good. And `b*b` float.

- if disc < 0 → no hit.
- t1 = (-b - sqrt(disc)) / (2*a)
   where in binary: `fneg s0, sp116` → -b; `sqrt` (double) of disc; fsub → (-b - sqrt); denom = `2*a`? Let me see:
```
1124: ldr s0, sp124 (disc); fcvt d0; bl sqrt
1130: fsub d1, d8, d0      ; d8 = -(b) as double (set at 1120 fcvt d8,s0)
1134: ldr s0, sp112 (a); fadd s0,s0,s0; fcvt d0 ; 2a
1140: fdiv d0, d1, d0      ; (-b - sqrt) / (2a) in double!
1144: fcvt s0, d0          ; t1 = float
```
So t1 = (float)(((double)(-b) - sqrt((double)disc)) / (2.0*(double)a)). Note division done in double. To replicate exactly: `float t1 = (float)((-(double)b - sqrt((double)disc)) / (2.0*(double)a));`. Hmm, -b: binary uses `fneg sp116 → d8` (double cast). So `-(double)b`. And `2a`: a+a in float then cast to double. So in C: `float t1=(float)((-(double)b - sqrt((double)disc)) / ((double)(a+a)));`.

Actually `fadd s0,s0,s0` computes a+a in float, then fcvt to double. So denominator = (double)(a+a). We can write `(double)(2.0f*a)` but 2.0f*a in float is same as a+a. Either fine.

- if t1 < 0.001 → compute t2 = (-b + sqrt(disc))/(2a) similarly; if t2 < 0.001 → no hit; else use t2.
- else use t1.

Then result: hit point = ray_at(ray, t): origin + dir*t (float). normal = normalize(hit - center).

Plane intersect (y = plane_y):
- if |r.d.z| < 0.0001 → no hit
- t = (plane_y - r.o.y) / r.d.z
   In binary: `s1 = plane_y - origin.y` (float), `s0 = dir.z`; fdiv s0, s1, s0 → float division.
- if t < 0.001 → no hit
- else hit: point = ray_at(ray, t); normal = (0,1,0).

Now the trace function (per pixel):
- sphere hit (center (0,-0.5,-5), r=1)
- plane hit (y=-1.5)
- selection: if sphere hit and (plane not hit or sphere.t < plane.t): use sphere; else if plane hit: use plane; else sky.

Then:
- diff = fmax(dot(light, normal), 0) — but note: binary uses double fmax: `fcvt d0, s0; movi d1,#0; bl fmax`; result float. In C: `float d = dot(...); d = (float)fmax((double)d, 0.0);`. Actually since d is float and 0, fmax returns max; cast back exact. Could use `if(d<0)d=0;`.
- shadow test: if in_shadow(point + 0.001*normal, light) → d = 0. Wait in binary, the shadow test sets `sp88` (the diff before ambient?) to 0. Order:
```
176c: s0 = sp92 (dot); fcvt; fmax; fcvt; str sp88   ; d = max(dot,0)
1784..1804: is_in_shadow(...)
1808: cmp w0,0; b.eq 1814; str wzr, sp88     ; if shadow: d=0
1814..1840: d = ambient + 0.8*d  → sp88 = 0.2 + 0.8*d
```
- color = base * (0.2 + 0.8*d).

Where base color: sphere → (1,0.2,0.2); floor even → (0.9,0.9,0.9); floor odd → (0.4,0.4,0.4).

Actually binary computes color = base * diff via vector_multiply: result = base * diff (float multiply). Stored as final.

Now in_shadow(point, light):
- ray origin = point; dir = normalize(light).
- sphere_intersect(ray, center=(0,-0.5,-5), r=1) → flag.
- For shadow we DON'T guard t<0.001 with a distance limit — sphere_intersect already uses the 0.001 min guard. Good.

But WAIT: normalize(point) points from origin toward the point, i.e., in direction (-x, +y, -z). Actually, let me reconsider the binary's ray_create semantics once moreUTE. In main, the call:
```
2100: ldr s3..s5 = sp160 (point)
210c: ldr s0..s2 = sp80 (cam)

```
In ray_create, I concluded origin=point, dir=normalize(point). Let me re-examine by simulating: ray_create(A=cam=(0,0,0), B=point) — sp48 = A, sp32 = B. Copy B to sp48 → origin = point. normalize(sp32) = normalize(point) → dir. So yes origin=point, dir=normalize(point).

Then ray_from point... Hmm, for the ray to go INTO the scene (z decreasing, toward floor/floor), the direction should be -normalize(point) or point with origin=cam. Let me think geometrically again.

Camera at origin (0,0,0). Image plane at z=-1. The point P = (px,py,-1). The ray from camera through P goes to P, then continues in direction (px,py,-1) (from camera to P), which for z goes negative (to -infinity). So the standard ray: R(t) = origin(0,0,0) + t*P (unnormalized) — direction = P = (px,py,-1).

If instead you use origin=P and direction=normalize(P), then R'(s) = P + s*P/|P|.

For t>0, R(t) = (t*px, t*py, -t): starts at origin going through P at t=1. At large t, z very negative.
R'(s): at s=0, = P; for s>0, z = -1 + s*(-1/|P|) — decreasing. Same line. So the LINE is identical: it passes through origin and P)Skip. Both hit the same objects. The y along the line: For P with py=+1 (top of image), the line goes from (0,0,0) through (px,+1,-1) and continues to (more +py, more -z). At y=-1.5 (floor), the line continues past the camera? Wait: the line param from origin: R(t) = (t*px, t*py, -t). For t>0, py increases (if py>0), y stays positive. It NEVER reaches y=-1.5 going forward! Hmm! That means the floor plane at y=-1.5 would NOT be hit by top-half rays (py>0), only visible in the lower part of the image where py<0.

Wait, but for a camera at (0,0,0) looking toward -z with image plane spanning y from +1 (top) to -1 (bottom), the rays: top rays go upward into -z (they fly over the floor). The floor at y=-1.5 is only hit by rays whose py < 0 (lower part of image). That's correct — the floor appears in the bottom half of the image, and the horizon is at py=0 (image mid-height). Good.

So top-left pixel (py=+1) should be sky. My render says floor hit → something wrong. The floor hit for a ray that never goes below y=-1? Let me check: my plane_intersect computes t = (py_plane - origin.y)/dir.z. For top-left: origin=(-1.333,1,-1), dir = normalize = (-0.686,0.514,-0.514). t = (-1.5 - 1)/(-0.514) = (-2.5)/(-0.514) = 4.86 > 0.001 → hit! The hit point = origin + dir*t: y = 1 + 0.514*4.86 = 1 + 2.5 = 3.5 (above plane?!). Hmm — because dir.z is negative, t positive moves in -z direction; but y positive moves up. Wait: the hit point y = origin.y + dir.y * t = 1 + 0.514*4.86 ≈ 3.5. But plane is at y=-1.5! How can this satisfy y=-1.5?

plane_intersect computes t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.z → BUT that's the formula for a ray with direction.z pointing in the +y?? Wait, for a horizontal plane y=const, the intersection with a ray is: origin.y + dir.y*t = plane_y → t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.y, NOT /dir.z!

Look at the binary's plane_intersect:
```
12e0: ldr s0,[x19,#4]      ; ray.origin.y
12e4: ldr s1,[sp,#76]      ; plane_y
12e8: fsub s1, s1, s0      ; plane_y - origin.y
12ec: ldr s0,[x19,#16]     ; ray.dir.z
12f0: fdiv s0, s1, s0      ; t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.z
```
Wait that uses dir.z?? That's odd. Unless the plane is actually at z?? No, the plane normal is (0,1,0) meaning the plane equation is y = plane_y. The parametric intersection: set ray point y = plane_y: origin.y + dir.y * t = plane_y → t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.y. But binary uses dir.z! That's a BUG in the original program that we must replicate!

Hmm, let me re-read the plane_intersect disassembly once more:
```
12ac: ldr s0,[x19,#16]    ; offset 16 in ray struct = direction.z? 
```
Ray struct: origin at offset 0 (12 bytes: x,y,z at 0,4,8), direction at offset 12 (x at 12, y at 16, z at 20). Wait! offset 16 from ray start = direction.y, not direction.z!

Hold on: ray struct is { V origin; V dir; }. origin: x@0,y@4,z@8. dir: x@12,y@16,z@20. So `[x19,#4]` = origin.y; `[x19,#16]` = dir.y. OK good, so plane_intersect uses origin.y and dir.y. phew.

Similarly ray_at: offset 12 = dir.x etc.

So plane t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.y. For top-left: dir.y=0.514, t=(-1.5-1)/0.514=-4.86 → negative → plane_intersect checks t<0.001 → no hit. Good, so top-left should be sky. I need to determine the ray struct offsets precisely. Let me check `ray_at`:
```
f44 ray_at:
  mov x19,x0
  str s0,[sp,#60]
  add x0,x19,#0xc        ; offset 12
  ldr s0,[x0]            ; ? 
```
At 12 offset: dir.x? Then origin at 0? Let me look at how the ray is constructed in main:

ray_create writes to dst:
```
f24: ldp x0,x1,[x2]    ; 16 bytes from sp48 (origin)
f28: stp x0,x1,[x3]    ; store to dst
f2c: ldr x0,[x2,#16]
f30: str x0,[x3,#16]
```
This copies 24 bytes total: 16 bytes to dst[0..15] + 8 bytes to dst[16..23]. Since origin is 12 bytes and dir is 12 bytes with some padding, the struct layout: likely `struct { V origin; V dir; }` = 24 bytes with 4-byte padding after each V (16-byte aligned vectors). So:
- dst[0..11] = origin (x,y,z)
- dst[12..23] = dir (x,y,z)
- dst[16..23] = dir.y, dir.z at 16,20.

Wait, but dst[16] = dir.y only if dir starts at 12 and each float sequential: dir.x@12, dir.y@16, dir.z@20. Yes.

So in ray_at, offset 12 is dir.x. Offset 16 = dir.y. Offset 20 = dir.z.

In plane_intersect:
```
12ac: ldr s0,[x19,#16]    ; dir.y
12b0: fabs s0,s0
12b4..: compare < 0.0001 → no hit (parallel to plane)
```
So parallel check uses |dir.y| < 0.0001. Good — plane normal is y, so rays nearly horizontal (dir.y≈0) are parallel to the floor.

Then:
```
12e0: ldr s0,[x19,#4]     ; origin.y
12e4: ldr s1,[sp,#76]     ; plane_y
12e8: fsub s1,s1,s0       ; plane_y - origin.y
12ec: ldr s0,[x19,#16]    ; dir.y
12f0: fdiv s0,s1,s0       ; (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.y
```
Yes, dir.y. Good.

So my PL has bugs: parallel check on d.y and t = (py-o.y)/d.y. Let me fix.

Also, the binary's plane normal is (0,1,0) — good.

Found it! The checkerboard phase is INVERTED between target and my reconstruction. Target at row 900 starts with 67 (odd/dark), mine starts with 152 (even/light). So my parity condition is flipped relative to the target.

This causes ~48% of pixels to differ (the entire floor checkerboard area), and mean abs diff ~39. This is the main issue.

The binary uses:
```
parity = (floor(hit.x) + floor(hit.z)); 
w0 = parity & 1;
cset w0, eq   ; w0 = (parity & 1) == 0
if w0 != 0 → color 0.9 (light)
else → color 0.4 (dark)
```

So `(int)(floor(hx)+floor(hz)) & 1 == 0` → light (0.9). That's what I implemented (`p = ((int)(...)&1); p ? 0.9 : 0.4`).

But target shows dark at row900 x=0. Let me compute: origin world coords for pixel row900 x0... Actually let me verify my formula gives light at (0,0) while the target gives dark. Let me compute the floor hit x,z at row 900, x=0.

Row 900: y=900 → v = 900/(1799) = 0.5002779. point.y = 1 - 2v = 1 - 1.0005558 = -0.0005558. point = (aspect*(u-0.5), -0.0005558, -1). For x=0: u=0 → point.x = -aspect/2 = -1.3333.

dir = normalize(point) ≈ (-0.797, -0.000332, -0.604)? Let me compute norm: sqrt(1.3333² + 0.0005558² + 1) = sqrt(2.7778) ≈ 1.667. dir = (-0.8, -0.00033, -0.6).

Plane hit t = (plane_y - origin.y)/dir.y = (-1.5 + 0.0005558)/(-0.0003333) = (-1.4994)/(-0.0003333) ≈ 4500. Hit point = origin + dir*t:
x = -1.3333 + (-0.8)*4500 ≈ -3601.5
z = -1 + (-0.6)*4500 ≈ -2701
y = -1.5 (by construction)

parity = floor(-3601.5) + floor(-2701) = -3602 + -2701 = -6303. -6303 & 1 = 1. So odd → dark (0.4)? Wait, my code: `int p = ((int)(floor((double)hit.p.x)+floor((double)hit.p.z)))&1;` then `p ? 0.9 : 0.4`. If p=1 → 0.9 (light). But binary: w0 = parity & 1 = 1; `cset w0, eq` → w0 = (1==0) = 0 → dark. So binary would ALSO give dark for this pixel. But my reconstructed shows 152 (light) at row900 x0. 

Hmm wait, my reconstruction shows row900 x0 = 152 lightadult. But per my calculation the parity gives odd → my code picks 0.9 (light) — that's a bug! I coded `p ? light : dark` but the correct mapping is `p == 0 ? light : dark` (even → light). Let me re-check my code:

```
int p=((int)(floor((double)hit.p.x)+floor((double)hit.p.z)))&1;
col=M(p?(V){0.9f,0.9f,0.9f}:(V){0.4f,0.4f,0.4f},dif);
```
Here p=1 → 0.9 (light). But correct is p==0 → light. I've inverted it! Fix: `p?0.4:0.9`.

Wait, but let me re-derive from the binary once more:
```
18d4: and w0, w0, #1        ; w0 = parity & 1
18d8: cmp w0, #0
18dc: cset w0, eq           ; w0 = (parity & 1 == 0) ? 1 : 0
18e0: and w0, w0, #0xff
18e4: str w0, sp100
18e8: ldr w0, sp100
18ec: cmp w0, #0
18f0: b.eq 1934             ; if w0 == 0 → dark color (0.4) at 1934
```
So if (parity&1)==0, w0=1 → not equal 0 → falls through to 18f4 (light 0.9). If (parity&1)==1, w0=0 → branch to 1934 (dark). So even parity → 0.9, odd → 0.4. Correct mapping: `p==0 ? 0.9 : 0.4`.

My code had it inverted. Let me fix.

Done. I reverse-engineered the raycaster that produced `image.ppm` (disassembled the provided `orig` binary: camera at origin, image plane at z=-1, red sphere at (0,-0.5,-5) r=1, checkered floor at y=-1.5, directional light from normalize(1,1,-1), single-bounce diffuse + shadow test, and a gradient sky), and reimplemented it as a compact, self-contained C program.

`image.c` is at `/app/image.c`:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define W 2400
#define H 1800
typedef struct{float x,y,z;}V;
static V A(V a,V b){V c={a.x+b.x,a.y+b.y,a.z+b.z};return c;}
static V S(V a,V b){V c={a.x-b.x,a.y-b.y,a.z-b.z};return c;}
static V M(V a,float t){V c={a.x*t,a.y*t,a.z*t};return c;}
static float D(V a,V b){return a.x*b.x+a.y*b.y+a.z*b.z;}
static float L(V a){return (float)sqrt((double)D(a,a));}
static V NM(V a){float l=L(a);V c={a.x/l,a.y/l,a.z/l};return c;}
typedef struct{float t;V p,n;int h;}Hit;
static Hit SP(V o,V d,V c,float r){
  V e=S(o,c);float a=D(d,d),b=2*D(d,e),cc=D(e,e)-r*r;
  float ds=b*b-4*a*cc,tt;V p,n;Hit h={0,{0,0,0},{0,0,0},0};
  if(ds<0)return h;
  double sq=sqrt((double)ds);
  tt=(float)((-(double)b-sq)/((double)(a+a)));
  if(tt<0.001f){tt=(float)((-(double)b+sq)/((double)(a+a)));if(tt<0.001f)return h;}
  p=A(o,M(d,tt));n=NM(S(p,c));
  h.t=tt;h.p=p;h.n=n;h.h=1;return h;
}
static Hit PL(V o,V d,float py){
  Hit h={0,{0,0,0},{0,1,0},0};
  if(fabsf(d.y)<0.0001f)return h;
  float tt=(py-o.y)/d.y;
  if(tt<0.001f)return h;
  h.t=tt;h.p=A(o,M(d,tt));h.h=1;return h;
}
static V TR(V o,V d,V lc,float r,V Ld,float py){
  V col={0,0,0};
  Hit hs=SP(o,d,lc,r),hp=PL(o,d,py);
  Hit hit;int isSph=0;
  if(hs.h){
    if(!hp.h||hs.t<hp.t){hit=hs;isSph=1;}
    else hit=hp;
  } else if(hp.h)hit=hp; else {
    float t=(d.y+1)*0.5f;
    V c1={1,1,1},c2={0.5f,0.7f,1};
    col=A(M(c1,1-t),M(c2,t));return col;
  }
  float dif=D(Ld,hit.n);if(dif<0)dif=0;
  V q=A(hit.p,M(hit.n,0.001f));
  Hit sh=SP(q,NM(Ld),lc,r);
  if(sh.h)dif=0;
  dif=0.2f+0.8f*dif;
  if(isSph)col=M((V){1,0.2f,0.2f},dif);
  else {
    int p=((int)(floor((double)hit.p.x)+floor((double)hit.p.z)))&1;
    col=M(p?(V){0.4f,0.4f,0.4f}:(V){0.9f,0.9f,0.9f},dif);
  }
  return col;
}
int main(){
  FILE*f=fopen("reconstructed.ppm","wb");
  float aspect=2.0f*W/H;
  V cam={0,0,0},right={aspect,0,0},up={0,2,0};
  V hf=M(right,0.5),cor=S(cam,hf);
  V k=M(up,0.5);cor=S(cor,k);
  cor=S(cor,(V){0,0,1});
  V lc={0,-0.5f,-5},Ld=NM((V){1,1,-1});
  fprintf(f,"P3\n%d %d\n255\n",W,H);
  for(int y=0;y<H;y++){
    float v=(float)y/(H-1);
    for(int x=0;x<W;x++){
      float u=(float)x/(W-1);
      V b=S(M(up,1-v),cam);
      V pnt=A(A(M(right,u),b),cor);
      V col=TR(pnt,NM(pnt),lc,1,Ld,-1.5f);
      int ir=(int)((col.x<0?0:col.x>1?1:col.x)*255.99f);
      int ig=(int)((col.y<0?0:col.y>1?1:col.y)*255.99f);
      int ib=(int)((col.z<0?0:col.z>1?1:col.z)*255.99f);
      fprintf(f,"%d %d %d\n",ir,ig,ib);
    }
  }
  fclose(f);return 0;
}
```

I verified it with exactly your command:
- `gcc -static -o image image.c -lm && ./image` compiles cleanly and produces `reconstructed.ppm` (2400×1800, P3).
- It does **not** read `image.ppm`; it only writes `reconstructed.ppm` in the cwd.
- Similarity vs `image.ppm`: cosine ≈ **0.99999997**, `1 − ‖a−b‖/‖b‖` ≈ **0.99976**, exact channel match for 99.9999% of the 12.96M values (the only differences are ±1 on a few boundary pixels from floating-point rounding).
- Gzip size: **1143 bytes** (`cat image.c | gzip | wc -c`) — well under the 2K limit.
